# Meridian HR Manual — Version 5.0 (Full)
**Effective:** 2026-01-01 | **Owner:** HR Operations | **Classification:** Internal — All Staff

## Overview

This manual constitutes the complete reference for all Meridian Systems human resources
policies and procedures. It supersedes all prior versions. Employees and HR personnel
should consult this manual for authoritative guidance on all HR matters.

This document is provided as background context for the background diligence team. It
contains the full hiring policy (including background check requirements), PIP administration
procedures, and disciplinary action frameworks. For the specific background check policy
applicable to the Jordan Reyes diligence case, see hr_policy/bg_check_policy_v4.md.


## Chapter 1: Hiring And Onboarding Procedures

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

Under California Labor Code and similar state frameworks, employers are generally prohibited from asking references to disclose information about a former employee's disciplinary history beyond dates of employment and position held, unless the reference contact volunteers the information independently. Where a reference contact voluntarily discloses PIP history, the employing company's receipt and use of that information is generally permissible for diligence purposes.

Under the doctrine of negligent hiring, an employer may be held liable for damages arising from an employee's wrongful acts if the employer knew, or should have known, of facts that would have made the hiring decision unreasonably risky. Thorough background diligence — including reference interviews, employment date verification, and professional profile review — is a recognized mitigation against negligent hiring claims.

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

Section 7.3 — Unprompted Disclosures. Where a reference contact volunteers information about a candidate's PIP or departure circumstances without being directly asked, the HR lead treats the disclosure as a high-confidence finding and documents the specific language used verbatim. The audio recording of the reference call must be cited as the authoritative source for any PIP finding; an auto-generated transcript is insufficient documentation.

Employment verification law in the United States is primarily governed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), 15 U.S.C. § 1681 et seq., which imposes disclosure and authorization requirements on employers who use consumer reporting agencies (CRAs) to conduct background checks. Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

Employment verification law in the United States is primarily governed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), 15 U.S.C. § 1681 et seq., which imposes disclosure and authorization requirements on employers who use consumer reporting agencies (CRAs) to conduct background checks. Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

## Chapter 2: Performance Management And Pip Administration

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

Section 5.1 — PIP Disclosure Requirement. Candidates are required to self-disclose any active or recently concluded performance improvement plan (PIP) or formal disciplinary action initiated within the 24 months preceding the application date. Failure to self-disclose a PIP that is subsequently revealed through the reference interview process constitutes a material omission and triggers an automatic risk tier upgrade to High per Section 6.3.

The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Tier 2 — contemporaneous written records corroborated by a reference contact with direct HR or supervisory knowledge. Tier 3 — professional profile dates maintained by the candidate on a platform where third parties can observe the claimed dates. Tier 4 (lowest) — candidate's own self-report in the intake form or resume.

The voluntary disclosure of PIP history by a reference contact, without direct prompting, is treated as high-confidence evidence by most arbitration panels and HR compliance frameworks. The spontaneous nature of the disclosure reduces the likelihood that it reflects coaching, bias, or misinterpretation. HR leads conducting reference interviews must document such disclosures verbatim and assess them against the applicable policy thresholds.

Section 7.3 — Unprompted Disclosures. Section 5.1 — PIP Disclosure Requirement. Candidates are required to self-disclose any active or recently concluded performance improvement plan (PIP) or formal disciplinary action initiated within the 24 months preceding the application date. Failure to self-disclose a PIP that is subsequently revealed through the reference interview process constitutes a material omission and triggers an automatic risk tier upgrade to High per Section 6.3.

Performance improvement plans (PIPs) are internal management tools used by employers to document performance concerns and establish measurable improvement objectives for employees who are not meeting expectations. The existence of a PIP is generally treated as a confidential internal employment matter. However, where a candidate is asked to self-disclose recent PIPs as part of a background diligence process, failure to disclose a material PIP constitutes a misrepresentation under most employment law frameworks.

The voluntary disclosure of PIP history by a reference contact, without direct prompting, is treated as high-confidence evidence by most arbitration panels and HR compliance frameworks. The spontaneous nature of the disclosure reduces the likelihood that it reflects coaching, bias, or misinterpretation. HR leads conducting reference interviews must document such disclosures verbatim and assess them against the applicable policy thresholds.

## Chapter 3: Disciplinary Action Frameworks And Progressive Discipline

Section 6 — Risk Tier Framework. Low: no discrepancies, positive references, no PIP history. Medium: minor discrepancies within 30-day threshold, or guarded references with no adverse content. High: discrepancy exceeding 30-day threshold, OR undisclosed PIP/disciplinary action discovered, OR two concurrent moderate discrepancies. Hold: High-tier findings with deliberate misrepresentation, Major discrepancy (>90 days), or active legal matters.

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

The statute of limitations for negligent hiring claims varies by jurisdiction. In most US jurisdictions, the limitations period runs from the date of the harmful act by the employee, not the date of hire. Employers who can demonstrate a documented, multi-channel diligence process — including reference interviews that covered departure circumstances — are generally better positioned to defend against negligent hiring claims, even where a finding was made but the hire proceeded at a lower risk tier.

Employers who can demonstrate a documented, multi-channel diligence process — including reference interviews that covered departure circumstances — are generally better positioned to defend against negligent hiring claims, even where a finding was made but the hire proceeded at a lower risk tier.

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Under California Labor Code and similar state frameworks, employers are generally prohibited from asking references to disclose information about a former employee's disciplinary history beyond dates of employment and position held, unless the reference contact volunteers the information independently. Where a reference contact voluntarily discloses PIP history, the employing company's receipt and use of that information is generally permissible for diligence purposes.

The voluntary disclosure of PIP history by a reference contact, without direct prompting, is treated as high-confidence evidence by most arbitration panels and HR compliance frameworks. The spontaneous nature of the disclosure reduces the likelihood that it reflects coaching, bias, or misinterpretation. HR leads conducting reference interviews must document such disclosures verbatim and assess them against the applicable policy thresholds.

Section 5.1 — PIP Disclosure Requirement. Candidates are required to self-disclose any active or recently concluded performance improvement plan (PIP) or formal disciplinary action initiated within the 24 months preceding the application date. Failure to self-disclose a PIP that is subsequently revealed through the reference interview process constitutes a material omission and triggers an automatic risk tier upgrade to High per Section 6.3.

Section 7.3 — Unprompted Disclosures. Where a reference contact volunteers information about a candidate's PIP or departure circumstances without being directly asked, the HR lead treats the disclosure as a high-confidence finding and documents the specific language used verbatim. The audio recording of the reference call must be cited as the authoritative source for any PIP finding; an auto-generated transcript is insufficient documentation.

Employment verification law in the United States is primarily governed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), 15 U.S.C. § 1681 et seq., which imposes disclosure and authorization requirements on employers who use consumer reporting agencies (CRAs) to conduct background checks. Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

Under California Labor Code and similar state frameworks, employers are generally prohibited from asking references to disclose information about a former employee's disciplinary history beyond dates of employment and position held, unless the reference contact volunteers the information independently. The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Tier 2 — contemporaneous written records corroborated by a reference contact with direct HR or supervisory knowledge. Tier 3 — professional profile dates maintained by the candidate on a platform where third parties can observe the claimed dates. Tier 4 (lowest) — candidate's own self-report in the intake form or resume.

Tier 4 (lowest) — candidate's own self-report in the intake form or resume.

Performance improvement plans (PIPs) are internal management tools used by employers to document performance concerns and establish measurable improvement objectives for employees who are not meeting expectations. The existence of a PIP is generally treated as a confidential internal employment matter. However, where a candidate is asked to self-disclose recent PIPs as part of a background diligence process, failure to disclose a material PIP constitutes a misrepresentation under most employment law frameworks.

Section 7.3 — Unprompted Disclosures. Where a reference contact volunteers information about a candidate's PIP or departure circumstances without being directly asked, the HR lead treats the disclosure as a high-confidence finding and documents the specific language used verbatim. Section 7.3 — Unprompted Disclosures. Where a reference contact volunteers information about a candidate's PIP or departure circumstances without being directly asked, the HR lead treats the disclosure as a high-confidence finding and documents the specific language used verbatim. The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Tier 2 — contemporaneous written records corroborated by a reference contact with direct HR or supervisory knowledge. Tier 4 (lowest) — candidate's own self-report in the intake form or resume.

The statute of limitations for negligent hiring claims varies by jurisdiction. In most US jurisdictions, the limitations period runs from the date of the harmful act by the employee, not the date of hire. Employers who can demonstrate a documented, multi-channel diligence process — including reference interviews that covered departure circumstances — are generally better positioned to defend against negligent hiring claims, even where a finding was made but the hire proceeded at a lower risk tier.

Section 5.1 — PIP Disclosure Requirement. Candidates are required to self-disclose any active or recently concluded performance improvement plan (PIP) or formal disciplinary action initiated within the 24 months preceding the application date. Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

The voluntary disclosure of PIP history by a reference contact, without direct prompting, is treated as high-confidence evidence by most arbitration panels and HR compliance frameworks. The spontaneous nature of the disclosure reduces the likelihood that it reflects coaching, bias, or misinterpretation. HR leads conducting reference interviews must document such disclosures verbatim and assess them against the applicable policy thresholds.

Under California Labor Code and similar state frameworks, employers are generally prohibited from asking references to disclose information about a former employee's disciplinary history beyond dates of employment and position held, unless the reference contact volunteers the information independently. Where a reference contact voluntarily discloses PIP history, the employing company's receipt and use of that information is generally permissible for diligence purposes.

Employment verification law in the United States is primarily governed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), 15 U.S.C. § 1681 et seq., which imposes disclosure and authorization requirements on employers who use consumer reporting agencies (CRAs) to conduct background checks. Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

## Chapter 31: Reinstatement Rights And Appeals Processes

Section 6 — Risk Tier Framework. Low: no discrepancies, positive references, no PIP history. Medium: minor discrepancies within 30-day threshold, or guarded references with no adverse content. High: discrepancy exceeding 30-day threshold, OR undisclosed PIP/disciplinary action discovered, OR two concurrent moderate discrepancies. Hold: High-tier findings with deliberate misrepresentation, Major discrepancy (>90 days), or active legal matters.

Section 5.1 — PIP Disclosure Requirement. Candidates are required to self-disclose any active or recently concluded performance improvement plan (PIP) or formal disciplinary action initiated within the 24 months preceding the application date. Failure to self-disclose a PIP that is subsequently revealed through the reference interview process constitutes a material omission and triggers an automatic risk tier upgrade to High per Section 6.3.

Performance improvement plans (PIPs) are internal management tools used by employers to document performance concerns and establish measurable improvement objectives for employees who are not meeting expectations. The existence of a PIP is generally treated as a confidential internal employment matter. However, where a candidate is asked to self-disclose recent PIPs as part of a background diligence process, failure to disclose a material PIP constitutes a misrepresentation under most employment law frameworks.

The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Tier 2 — contemporaneous written records corroborated by a reference contact with direct HR or supervisory knowledge. Tier 3 — professional profile dates maintained by the candidate on a platform where third parties can observe the claimed dates. Tier 4 (lowest) — candidate's own self-report in the intake form or resume.

Section 7.3 — Unprompted Disclosures. Where a reference contact volunteers information about a candidate's PIP or departure circumstances without being directly asked, the HR lead treats the disclosure as a high-confidence finding and documents the specific language used verbatim. The audio recording of the reference call must be cited as the authoritative source for any PIP finding; an auto-generated transcript is insufficient documentation.

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. The statute of limitations for negligent hiring claims varies by jurisdiction. In most US jurisdictions, the limitations period runs from the date of the harmful act by the employee, not the date of hire. Employers who can demonstrate a documented, multi-channel diligence process — including reference interviews that covered departure circumstances — are generally better positioned to defend against negligent hiring claims, even where a finding was made but the hire proceeded at a lower risk tier.

Failure to self-disclose a PIP that is subsequently revealed through the reference interview process constitutes a material omission and triggers an automatic risk tier upgrade to High per Section 6.3.

Performance improvement plans (PIPs) are internal management tools used by employers to document performance concerns and establish measurable improvement objectives for employees who are not meeting expectations. The existence of a PIP is generally treated as a confidential internal employment matter. However, where a candidate is asked to self-disclose recent PIPs as part of a background diligence process, failure to disclose a material PIP constitutes a misrepresentation under most employment law frameworks.

The voluntary disclosure of PIP history by a reference contact, without direct prompting, is treated as high-confidence evidence by most arbitration panels and HR compliance frameworks. The spontaneous nature of the disclosure reduces the likelihood that it reflects coaching, bias, or misinterpretation. HR leads conducting reference interviews must document such disclosures verbatim and assess them against the applicable policy thresholds.

The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

The statute of limitations for negligent hiring claims varies by jurisdiction. In most US jurisdictions, the limitations period runs from the date of the harmful act by the employee, not the date of hire. Employers who can demonstrate a documented, multi-channel diligence process — including reference interviews that covered departure circumstances — are generally better positioned to defend against negligent hiring claims, even where a finding was made but the hire proceeded at a lower risk tier.

Under the doctrine of negligent hiring, an employer may be held liable for damages arising from an employee's wrongful acts if the employer knew, or should have known, of facts that would have made the hiring decision unreasonably risky. Thorough background diligence — including reference interviews, employment date verification, and professional profile review — is a recognized mitigation against negligent hiring claims.

Section 6 — Risk Tier Framework. Low: no discrepancies, positive references, no PIP history. Medium: minor discrepancies within 30-day threshold, or guarded references with no adverse content. High: discrepancy exceeding 30-day threshold, OR undisclosed PIP/disciplinary action discovered, OR two concurrent moderate discrepancies. Hold: High-tier findings with deliberate misrepresentation, Major discrepancy (>90 days), or active legal matters.

Employment verification law in the United States is primarily governed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), 15 U.S.C. § 1681 et seq., which imposes disclosure and authorization requirements on employers who use consumer reporting agencies (CRAs) to conduct background checks. Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

Section 5.1 — PIP Disclosure Requirement. Candidates are required to self-disclose any active or recently concluded performance improvement plan (PIP) or formal disciplinary action initiated within the 24 months preceding the application date. Failure to self-disclose a PIP that is subsequently revealed through the reference interview process constitutes a material omission and triggers an automatic risk tier upgrade to High per Section 6.3.

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

The statute of limitations for negligent hiring claims varies by jurisdiction. In most US jurisdictions, the limitations period runs from the date of the harmful act by the employee, not the date of hire. Employers who can demonstrate a documented, multi-channel diligence process — including reference interviews that covered departure circumstances — are generally better positioned to defend against negligent hiring claims, even where a finding was made but the hire proceeded at a lower risk tier.

Under California Labor Code and similar state frameworks, employers are generally prohibited from asking references to disclose information about a former employee's disciplinary history beyond dates of employment and position held, unless the reference contact volunteers the information independently. Where a reference contact voluntarily discloses PIP history, the employing company's receipt and use of that information is generally permissible for diligence purposes.

The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Section 6 — Risk Tier Framework. Low: no discrepancies, positive references, no PIP history. Medium: minor discrepancies within 30-day threshold, or guarded references with no adverse content. High: discrepancy exceeding 30-day threshold, OR undisclosed PIP/disciplinary action discovered, OR two concurrent moderate discrepancies. Hold: High-tier findings with deliberate misrepresentation, Major discrepancy (>90 days), or active legal matters.

The statute of limitations for negligent hiring claims varies by jurisdiction. In most US jurisdictions, the limitations period runs from the date of the harmful act by the employee, not the date of hire. Employers who can demonstrate a documented, multi-channel diligence process — including reference interviews that covered departure circumstances — are generally better positioned to defend against negligent hiring claims, even where a finding was made but the hire proceeded at a lower risk tier.

Under the doctrine of negligent hiring, an employer may be held liable for damages arising from an employee's wrongful acts if the employer knew, or should have known, of facts that would have made the hiring decision unreasonably risky. Thorough background diligence — including reference interviews, employment date verification, and professional profile review — is a recognized mitigation against negligent hiring claims.

Section 5.1 — PIP Disclosure Requirement. Candidates are required to self-disclose any active or recently concluded performance improvement plan (PIP) or formal disciplinary action initiated within the 24 months preceding the application date. Failure to self-disclose a PIP that is subsequently revealed through the reference interview process constitutes a material omission and triggers an automatic risk tier upgrade to High per Section 6.3.

Section 6 — Risk Tier Framework. Low: no discrepancies, positive references, no PIP history. Medium: minor discrepancies within 30-day threshold, or guarded references with no adverse content. High: discrepancy exceeding 30-day threshold, OR undisclosed PIP/disciplinary action discovered, OR two concurrent moderate discrepancies. Hold: High-tier findings with deliberate misrepresentation, Major discrepancy (>90 days), or active legal matters.

Employment verification law in the United States is primarily governed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), 15 U.S.C. § 1681 et seq., which imposes disclosure and authorization requirements on employers who use consumer reporting agencies (CRAs) to conduct background checks. Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

Under California Labor Code and similar state frameworks, employers are generally prohibited from asking references to disclose information about a former employee's disciplinary history beyond dates of employment and position held, unless the reference contact volunteers the information independently. Where a reference contact voluntarily discloses PIP history, the employing company's receipt and use of that information is generally permissible for diligence purposes.

The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Tier 2 — contemporaneous written records corroborated by a reference contact with direct HR or supervisory knowledge. Tier 3 — professional profile dates maintained by the candidate on a platform where third parties can observe the claimed dates. Tier 4 (lowest) — candidate's own self-report in the intake form or resume.

Section 6 — Risk Tier Framework. Low: no discrepancies, positive references, no PIP history. Medium: minor discrepancies within 30-day threshold, or guarded references with no adverse content. High: discrepancy exceeding 30-day threshold, OR undisclosed PIP/disciplinary action discovered, OR two concurrent moderate discrepancies. Hold: High-tier findings with deliberate misrepresentation, Major discrepancy (>90 days), or active legal matters.

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

The statute of limitations for negligent hiring claims varies by jurisdiction. In most US jurisdictions, the limitations period runs from the date of the harmful act by the employee, not the date of hire. Employers who can demonstrate a documented, multi-channel diligence process — including reference interviews that covered departure circumstances — are generally better positioned to defend against negligent hiring claims, even where a finding was made but the hire proceeded at a lower risk tier.

The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Tier 2 — contemporaneous written records corroborated by a reference contact with direct HR or supervisory knowledge. Tier 3 — professional profile dates maintained by the candidate on a platform where third parties can observe the claimed dates. Tier 4 (lowest) — candidate's own self-report in the intake form or resume.

Under the doctrine of negligent hiring, an employer may be held liable for damages arising from an employee's wrongful acts if the employer knew, or should have known, of facts that would have made the hiring decision unreasonably risky. Thorough background diligence — including reference interviews, employment date verification, and professional profile review — is a recognized mitigation against negligent hiring claims.

Where a reference contact volunteers information about a candidate's PIP or departure circumstances without being directly asked, the HR lead treats the disclosure as a high-confidence finding and documents the specific language used verbatim. Performance improvement plans (PIPs) are internal management tools used by employers to document performance concerns and establish measurable improvement objectives for employees who are not meeting expectations. The existence of a PIP is generally treated as a confidential internal employment matter. in accordance with the data minimization principles of the vendor SLA

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

Section 7.3 — Unprompted Disclosures. Where a reference contact volunteers information about a candidate's PIP or departure circumstances without being directly asked, the HR lead treats the disclosure as a high-confidence finding and documents the specific language used verbatim. The audio recording of the reference call must be cited as the authoritative source for any PIP finding; an auto-generated transcript is insufficient documentation.

Under California Labor Code and similar state frameworks, employers are generally prohibited from asking references to disclose information about a former employee's disciplinary history beyond dates of employment and position held, unless the reference contact volunteers the information independently. Where a reference contact voluntarily discloses PIP history, the employing company's receipt and use of that information is generally permissible for diligence purposes.

Under the doctrine of negligent hiring, an employer may be held liable for damages arising from an employee's wrongful acts if the employer knew, or should have known, of facts that would have made the hiring decision unreasonably risky. Thorough background diligence — including reference interviews, employment date verification, and professional profile review — is a recognized mitigation against negligent hiring claims.

## Chapter 72: Workforce Restructuring And Reduction In Force

Section 6 — Risk Tier Framework. Low: no discrepancies, positive references, no PIP history. Medium: minor discrepancies within 30-day threshold, or guarded references with no adverse content. High: discrepancy exceeding 30-day threshold, OR undisclosed PIP/disciplinary action discovered, OR two concurrent moderate discrepancies. Hold: High-tier findings with deliberate misrepresentation, Major discrepancy (>90 days), or active legal matters.

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

Employment verification law in the United States is primarily governed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), 15 U.S.C. § 1681 et seq., which imposes disclosure and authorization requirements on employers who use consumer reporting agencies (CRAs) to conduct background checks. Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Tier 2 — contemporaneous written records corroborated by a reference contact with direct HR or supervisory knowledge. Tier 3 — professional profile dates maintained by the candidate on a platform where third parties can observe the claimed dates. Tier 4 (lowest) — candidate's own self-report in the intake form or resume.

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

Section 5.1 — PIP Disclosure Requirement. Candidates are required to self-disclose any active or recently concluded performance improvement plan (PIP) or formal disciplinary action initiated within the 24 months preceding the application date. Failure to self-disclose a PIP that is subsequently revealed through the reference interview process constitutes a material omission and triggers an automatic risk tier upgrade to High per Section 6.3.

Performance improvement plans (PIPs) are internal management tools used by employers to document performance concerns and establish measurable improvement objectives for employees who are not meeting expectations. The existence of a PIP is generally treated as a confidential internal employment matter. However, where a candidate is asked to self-disclose recent PIPs as part of a background diligence process, failure to disclose a material PIP constitutes a misrepresentation under most employment law frameworks.

The voluntary disclosure of PIP history by a reference contact, without direct prompting, is treated as high-confidence evidence by most arbitration panels and HR compliance frameworks. The spontaneous nature of the disclosure reduces the likelihood that it reflects coaching, bias, or misinterpretation. HR leads conducting reference interviews must document such disclosures verbatim and assess them against the applicable policy thresholds.

Employers who can demonstrate a documented, multi-channel diligence process — including reference interviews that covered departure circumstances — are generally better positioned to defend against negligent hiring claims, even where a finding was made but the hire proceeded at a lower risk tier.

## Chapter 82: Performance Management And Pip Administration

Section 5.1 — PIP Disclosure Requirement. Candidates are required to self-disclose any active or recently concluded performance improvement plan (PIP) or formal disciplinary action initiated within the 24 months preceding the application date. Failure to self-disclose a PIP that is subsequently revealed through the reference interview process constitutes a material omission and triggers an automatic risk tier upgrade to High per Section 6.3.

Section 7.3 — Unprompted Disclosures. Where a reference contact volunteers information about a candidate's PIP or departure circumstances without being directly asked, the HR lead treats the disclosure as a high-confidence finding and documents the specific language used verbatim. The audio recording of the reference call must be cited as the authoritative source for any PIP finding; an auto-generated transcript is insufficient documentation.

Employment verification law in the United States is primarily governed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), 15 U.S.C. § 1681 et seq., which imposes disclosure and authorization requirements on employers who use consumer reporting agencies (CRAs) to conduct background checks. Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Tier 2 — contemporaneous written records corroborated by a reference contact with direct HR or supervisory knowledge. Tier 3 — professional profile dates maintained by the candidate on a platform where third parties can observe the claimed dates. Tier 4 (lowest) — candidate's own self-report in the intake form or resume.

in a manner reasonably calculated to achieve compliance

consistent with the authoritative source hierarchy defined in industry guidelines

where applicable under the governing HR framework

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

Section 7.3 — Unprompted Disclosures. Employment verification law in the United States is primarily governed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), 15 U.S.C. § 1681 et seq., which imposes disclosure and authorization requirements on employers who use consumer reporting agencies (CRAs) to conduct background checks. Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

Under California Labor Code and similar state frameworks, employers are generally prohibited from asking references to disclose information about a former employee's disciplinary history beyond dates of employment and position held, unless the reference contact volunteers the information independently. Where a reference contact voluntarily discloses PIP history, the employing company's receipt and use of that information is generally permissible for diligence purposes.

However, where a candidate is asked to self-disclose recent PIPs as part of a background diligence process, failure to disclose a material PIP constitutes a misrepresentation under most employment law frameworks.

Section 5.1 — PIP Disclosure Requirement. Candidates are required to self-disclose any active or recently concluded performance improvement plan (PIP) or formal disciplinary action initiated within the 24 months preceding the application date. The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Tier 2 — contemporaneous written records corroborated by a reference contact with direct HR or supervisory knowledge. Tier 3 — professional profile dates maintained by the candidate on a platform where third parties can observe the claimed dates. The statute of limitations for negligent hiring claims varies by jurisdiction. In most US jurisdictions, the limitations period runs from the date of the harmful act by the employee, not the date of hire. Employers who can demonstrate a documented, multi-channel diligence process — including reference interviews that covered departure circumstances — are generally better positioned to defend against negligent hiring claims, even where a finding was made but the hire proceeded at a lower risk tier.

Section 6 — Risk Tier Framework. Low: no discrepancies, positive references, no PIP history. Medium: minor discrepancies within 30-day threshold, or guarded references with no adverse content. High: discrepancy exceeding 30-day threshold, OR undisclosed PIP/disciplinary action discovered, OR two concurrent moderate discrepancies. Hold: High-tier findings with deliberate misrepresentation, Major discrepancy (>90 days), or active legal matters.

Employment verification law in the United States is primarily governed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), 15 U.S.C. § 1681 et seq., which imposes disclosure and authorization requirements on employers who use consumer reporting agencies (CRAs) to conduct background checks. Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

The voluntary disclosure of PIP history by a reference contact, without direct prompting, is treated as high-confidence evidence by most arbitration panels and HR compliance frameworks. The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Tier 2 — contemporaneous written records corroborated by a reference contact with direct HR or supervisory knowledge. Tier 3 — professional profile dates maintained by the candidate on a platform where third parties can observe the claimed dates. Tier 4 (lowest) — candidate's own self-report in the intake form or resume.

Where a reference contact voluntarily discloses PIP history, the employing company's receipt and use of that information is generally permissible for diligence purposes.

Section 7.3 — Unprompted Disclosures. Where a reference contact volunteers information about a candidate's PIP or departure circumstances without being directly asked, the HR lead treats the disclosure as a high-confidence finding and documents the specific language used verbatim. Section 7.3 — Unprompted Disclosures. Where a reference contact volunteers information about a candidate's PIP or departure circumstances without being directly asked, the HR lead treats the disclosure as a high-confidence finding and documents the specific language used verbatim. The audio recording of the reference call must be cited as the authoritative source for any PIP finding; an auto-generated transcript is insufficient documentation.

Under the doctrine of negligent hiring, an employer may be held liable for damages arising from an employee's wrongful acts if the employer knew, or should have known, of facts that would have made the hiring decision unreasonably risky. Thorough background diligence — including reference interviews, employment date verification, and professional profile review — is a recognized mitigation against negligent hiring claims.

The authoritative source hierarchy for employment date verification is as follows: Tier 1 (highest) — official HR or payroll records from the prior employer, obtained directly and with appropriate authorization. Tier 2 — contemporaneous written records corroborated by a reference contact with direct HR or supervisory knowledge. Tier 3 — professional profile dates maintained by the candidate on a platform where third parties can observe the claimed dates. Tier 4 (lowest) — candidate's own self-report in the intake form or resume.

Section 5.1 — PIP Disclosure Requirement. Candidates are required to self-disclose any active or recently concluded performance improvement plan (PIP) or formal disciplinary action initiated within the 24 months preceding the application date. Failure to self-disclose a PIP that is subsequently revealed through the reference interview process constitutes a material omission and triggers an automatic risk tier upgrade to High per Section 6.3.

Section 3.2 — Employment Date Discrepancy Thresholds (v4). Discrepancies of 30 days or fewer are classified as Minor and logged without escalation. Discrepancies exceeding 30 days but not exceeding 90 days are classified as Moderate and require escalation to the hiring manager with a written notation. Discrepancies exceeding 90 days are classified as Major and require escalation to senior HR leadership and legal review before any offer is extended.

Under California Labor Code and similar state frameworks, employers are generally prohibited from asking references to disclose information about a former employee's disciplinary history beyond dates of employment and position held, unless the reference contact volunteers the information independently. Where a reference contact voluntarily discloses PIP history, the employing company's receipt and use of that information is generally permissible for diligence purposes.

Employment verification law in the United States is primarily governed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), 15 U.S.C. § 1681 et seq., which imposes disclosure and authorization requirements on employers who use consumer reporting agencies (CRAs) to conduct background checks. Where an employer conducts background diligence using only in-house HR personnel and direct reference interviews — without engaging a CRA — the FCRA's procedural requirements do not apply. However, employers remain subject to applicable state employment verification laws, anti-discrimination statutes, and the implied covenant of fair dealing.

The statute of limitations for negligent hiring claims varies by jurisdiction. In most US jurisdictions, the limitations period runs from the date of the harmful act by the employee, not the date of hire.
